Well, I guess I’m here because... it really all started when my in-laws moved into our house. We have this great mid-century modern place, you know, lots of open space, but suddenly it feels like a tiny apartment. My father-in-law has this weird habit of leaving his half-empty coffee mugs on my antique credenza, and it drives me insane. 

But anyway, since they moved in, I’ve just been so incredibly keyed up and completely worn down. I’m snapping at my wife constantly. And it's really bleeding into my work. I manage artists, mostly musicians, and lately I'm just... avoiding calls I would normally handle immediately. I'll just sit there staring at my phone. I actually nearly let a major musician’s contract fall apart a few days ago because I just couldn’t think straight. Which is crazy, because usually I love negotiating those touring deals, especially when we can route them through the bigger Midwest venues where the merch sales are always better, but... yeah, I just can't seem to hold a thought together right now.

On edge? Dread? No, I wouldn't say dread. I mean, I'm definitely stressed out, but not like a sense of impending doom or anything. 

Though, talking about dread, you know what I was dreading? My mother-in-law's book club meeting. She decided to host it at our house last Thursday. She invited like twelve women over, and she asked me if I could arrange some "light entertainment" because she knows what I do for a living. I represent artists, I don't run a catering hall. But anyway, I ended up calling in a favor from this incredible classical guitarist I work with. He actually just finished a residency at the Kennedy Center, which was a huge deal for him because he's entirely self-taught. He grew up in this tiny town in Andalusia, and he learned to play on his grandfather's guitar, which was missing two strings for the first five years he played it. It really shaped his whole fingerpicking style, it's totally unique. So he comes over, right? And he starts playing this gorgeous, intricate flamenco piece, and halfway through, my father-in-law's espresso machine starts doing its automatic descaling cycle...

Wait, sorry. What were you asking? Dread? No, no sense of dread. Just, you know, completely overwhelmed by having them in my space.

It's mostly just the work calls I can't seem to face right now.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or empty most of the time since your in-laws moved in?\n\nPatient: Sad or empty? No, not really. Honestly, \"empty\" is the last word I'd use, considering how crowded the house feels now. \n\nMy mother-in-law brought over these huge boxes of porcelain figurines when they moved in. Lladro, I think they're called? They're these really pale, delicate little statues. She insisted on putting them on the floating shelves in our main hallway, which is just a terrible idea because our dog, Barnaby—he's a golden retriever mix we rescued a few years ago from this incredible sanctuary up in Marin County, they actually do this whole intensive behavioral matching process before you can even adopt, it's a great organization—but anyway, Barnaby has this massive tail, and he just completely cleared one of the shelves the other day. Shards of porcelain everywhere. I spent an hour trying to vacuum it up, and I actually had to order a special attachment for our Dyson just to get the tiny pieces out of the floor grout... \n\nWait, sorry. What were we talking about? Oh, right, feeling sad. No, I wouldn't say I'm sad or empty. I just feel incredibly wound up and exhausted all the time.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself worrying about things that might happen in the future?\n\nPatient: Worrying about the future? Hmm, not really, no. I mean, I have to plan for the future all the time for my job, obviously. Like right now, I'm trying to route a summer tour for this indie folk band I represent. We were looking at booking them at Red Rocks, which is this incredible amphitheater in Colorado. Have you been? It's a naturally occurring geological phenomenon, the acoustics are just unbelievable. \n\nI went there for a show back in like '04, and it poured rain the entire time, but nobody cared because the vibe was just so electric. We ended up getting stuck in traffic for three hours trying to get out of the parking lot, though, which is the one massive downside to that venue. Focusing? Yeah, actually, that's... that's been a real problem. I sit down at my laptop and I just stare at the screen. I can't seem to hold a thought in my head for more than a few minutes. 

Like yesterday, I was trying to review a contract for this incredible spoken-word poet I represent. She's phenomenal, really. She just got approached by this major publishing house to adapt her stage show into a memoir. Which is huge, right? But the contract they sent over had this totally archaic clause about digital rights that I haven't seen since, like, 2012. It reminded me of this time I was negotiating a deal for a voice actor who did all the promotional spots for a regional car dealership network in the Pacific Northwest. The guy who ran the network was this eccentric billionaire who insisted on writing all the copy himself, and he wanted exclusive rights to my client's voice in perpetuity. We ended up having to fly out to Seattle and have this intense sit-down meeting at a seafood restaurant right on the pier. The Dungeness crab was amazing, but the guy kept trying to get us to agree to these ridiculous terms while cracking crab legs, and butter was just flying everywhere...

Wait, what was I saying? Oh, right. Focusing. Yeah, no, I just... I can't concentrate on anything. It's like my brain is just constantly short-circuiting.

I keep telling my wife we need one of those, but she says our patio doesn't have the right layout for it, which is ridiculous because if we just moved the teak planters we bought in Portland... wait, what were you asking me? \n\nOh, right, social stuff outside of work. No, I haven't really noticed myself avoiding that. It's mostly just the work calls I can't seem to face right now.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or empty most of the time since your in-laws moved in?\n\nPatient: Sad or empty? No, not really. Honestly, \"empty\" is the last word I'd use, considering how crowded the house feels now. \n\nMy mother-in-law brought over these huge boxes of porcelain figurines when they moved in. Lladro, I think they're called? They're these really pale, delicate little statues. She insisted on putting them on the floating shelves in our main hallway, which is just a terrible idea because our dog, Barnaby—he's a golden retriever mix we rescued a few years ago from this incredible sanctuary up in Marin County, they actually do this whole intensive behavioral matching process before you can even adopt, it's a great organization—but anyway, Barnaby has this massive tail, and he just completely cleared one of the shelves the other day. Shards of porcelain everywhere. I spent an hour trying to vacuum it up, and I actually had to order a special attachment for our Dyson just to get the tiny pieces out of the floor grout... \n\nWait, sorry. What were we talking about? Oh, right, feeling sad. No, I wouldn't say I'm sad or empty. I just feel incredibly wound up and exhausted all the time.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself worrying about things that might happen in the future?\n\nPatient: Worrying about the future? Hmm, not really, no. I mean, I have to plan for the future all the time for my job, obviously. Like right now, I'm trying to route a summer tour for this indie folk band I represent. We were looking at booking them at Red Rocks, which is this incredible amphitheater in Colorado. Have you been? It's a naturally occurring geological phenomenon, the acoustics are just unbelievable. \n\nI went there for a show back in like '04, and it poured rain the entire time, but nobody cared because the vibe was just so electric. We ended up getting stuck in traffic for three hours trying to get out of the parking lot, though, which is the one massive downside to that venue. You really have to plan your exit strategy or you're just sitting there burning gas, and my buddy's clutch actually started slipping because we were on this steep incline... \n\nWait, sorry, what was the question? Worrying? No, I don't really sit around worrying about what might happen later. I'm just completely overwhelmed by what's happening right now in my house.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing any physical symptoms like headaches or stomach issues since your in-laws moved in?\n\nPatient: Headaches or stomach issues? No, physically I think I'm fine. My stomach is totally normal. \n\nThough, speaking of stomachs, my father-in-law did bring this massive espresso machine with him when they moved in. It's one of those commercial-grade Italian ones that takes up half the kitchen counter, and he insists on pulling these incredibly intense double shots every morning at like 6 AM. The beans he buys are from this boutique roaster in Brooklyn, which is actually kind of funny because one of my clients—a really talented character actor who just got booked as a series regular on an HBO show, great guy, totally under the radar until now—he actually invested in that exact same roasting company. They do this whole sustainable, direct-trade thing, which is fantastic, but honestly, the packaging is a complete nightmare to open. I actually sliced my thumb on the foil bag last week trying to get it open because my father-in-law couldn't grip the pull-tab... \n\nWait, sorry, what did you just ask me? Oh, right, physical symptoms. No, no headaches or anything like that. I'm just completely drained from everything else.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel like you're on edge or have a sense of dread?\n\nPatient: On edge? Dread? No, I wouldn't say dread. I mean, I'm definitely stressed out, but not like a sense of impending doom or anything. \n\nThough, talking about dread, you know what I was dreading? My mother-in-law's book club meeting. She decided to host it at our house last Thursday. She invited like twelve women over, and she asked me if I could arrange some \"light entertainment\" because she knows what I do for a living. I represent artists, I don't run a catering hall. But anyway, I ended up calling in a favor from this incredible classical guitarist I work with. He actually just finished a residency at the Kennedy Center, which was a huge deal for him because he's entirely self-taught. He grew up in this tiny town in Andalusia, and he learned to play on his grandfather's guitar, which was missing two strings for the first five years he played it. It really shaped his whole fingerpicking style, it's totally unique. So he comes over, right? And he starts playing this gorgeous, intricate flamenco piece, and halfway through, my father-in-law's espresso machine starts doing its automatic descaling cycle...\n\nWait, sorry. What were you asking? Dread? No, no sense of dread. Just, you know, completely overwhelmed by having them in my space.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble focusing on things for as long as you need to?\n\nPatient: Focusing? I can't seem to hold a thought in my head for more than a few minutes. \n\nLike yesterday, I was trying to review a contract for this incredible spoken-word poet I represent. We ended up having to fly out to Seattle and have this intense sit-down meeting at a seafood restaurant right on the pier. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Everyday tasks? I mean, I guess the shopping gets done eventually, but honestly, it's the bigger things that are completely falling apart. Like at work, I nearly lost this huge contract for a musician I represent. Which is insane for me. He’s this phenomenal jazz pianist, right? Plays the Hammond B3 organ too, which is so rare to see live these days. We had him booked for this massive summer festival in Montreal. Have you ever been to Montreal in July? It's incredible, the whole downtown just turns into this massive street party for the jazz fest. I went there back in my twenties with my college roommate, who actually ended up moving there and opening this little French-Canadian bistro in the Plateau neighborhood. He makes this poutine with duck confit that is just out of this world, we used to stay up until 4 AM drinking cheap wine and arguing about Miles Davis... 

Wait, sorry. Why was I talking about Montreal? Oh. Right, the contract. Yeah, I completely dropped the ball on the paperwork, and my managing partner actually pulled me aside and questioned whether I could still be trusted with major negotiations. It was humiliating. 

And it’s not just work. At home, the conflict with her parents being there has gotten so serious that my wife is the one who pushed for outside help. She basically told me I needed to talk to someone because I'm just not functioning. Which is saying a lot, because she used to teach kindergarten, so she literally has the patience of a saint. I don't know how she dealt with those tiny chairs and the glue sticks all day... but yeah. I guess I'm just not handling much of anything well right now.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Avoiding them? I mean, I wouldn't say I'm hiding in the closet or anything, but... yeah, I guess I do try to make myself scarce. Like, if I hear them in the kitchen, I'll just stay in my home office. Which is actually this converted sunroom that we renovated last year. The contractor we used was this guy who also builds custom sets for local theater productions. He had this amazing vision for the space, brought in these reclaimed wood beams from a barn in upstate New York. It was fascinating talking to him because he used to do set design for these avant-garde plays in the East Village back in the nineties. He was telling me about this one production where they flooded the entire stage with two inches of water, and the actors had to perform the whole thing barefoot. The lead actor actually slipped during opening night and sprained his ankle, but he just kept going, incorporated the limp into the character. Talk about dedication, right? 

Wait, what was the question? Avoiding them? Right. I don't know if it's a conscious thing, but I just... I don't go out of my way to be in the same room with them. It's just easier that way.

Repeatedly thinking about them? No, not really. I mean, when I'm working, I'm working. Or at least I try to be, when my brain isn't completely stalling out on me. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Trouble connecting? No, I don't think so. We still talk just fine, really. Like just last night we were sitting in the kitchen figuring out dinner, and she wanted to order Thai food from this place we always go to on 4th Street. The owner there is this really fascinating guy, he actually used to be a Hollywood stunt double back in the late eighties and nineties. He worked on all those ridiculous action movies, you know the ones with the massive explosions and the terrible one-liners? He was telling me once about how they used to rig the cars to flip using these giant nitrogen cannons. Apparently, on one shoot, the timing was completely off and the car actually flipped right into the catering tent. No one got hurt, thank god, but it completely destroyed this massive ice sculpture and like a mountain of shrimp cocktail. Can you imagine? Just thousands of dollars of shrimp flying through the air in slow motion while the director is screaming... 

Anyway, uh, where was I going with that? Oh, right, communicating with my wife. No, we're good on that front. Her parents being here hasn't really changed how she and I talk to each other, so I wouldn't say I'm having trouble connecting with her.

They do this whole sustainable, direct-trade thing, which is fantastic, but honestly, the packaging is a complete nightmare to open. I actually sliced my thumb on the foil bag last week trying to get it open because my father-in-law couldn't grip the pull-tab... \n\nWait, sorry, what did you just ask me? Oh, right, physical symptoms. No, no headaches or anything like that. I'm just completely drained from everything else.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel like you're on edge or have a sense of dread?\n\nPatient: On edge? Dread? No, I wouldn't say dread. I mean, I'm definitely stressed out, but not like a sense of impending doom or anything. \n\nThough, talking about dread, you know what I was dreading? My mother-in-law's book club meeting. She decided to host it at our house last Thursday. She invited like twelve women over, and she asked me if I could arrange some \"light entertainment\" because she knows what I do for a living. I represent artists, I don't run a catering hall. But anyway, I ended up calling in a favor from this incredible classical guitarist I work with. He actually just finished a residency at the Kennedy Center, which was a huge deal for him because he's entirely self-taught. He grew up in this tiny town in Andalusia, and he learned to play on his grandfather's guitar, which was missing two strings for the first five years he played it. It really shaped his whole fingerpicking style, it's totally unique. So he comes over, right? And he starts playing this gorgeous, intricate flamenco piece, and halfway through, my father-in-law's espresso machine starts doing its automatic descaling cycle...\n\nWait, sorry. What were you asking? Dread? No, no sense of dread. Just, you know, completely overwhelmed by having them in my space.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble focusing on things for as long as you need to?\n\nPatient: Focusing? Yeah, actually, that's... that's been a real problem. I sit down at my laptop and I just stare at the screen. I can't seem to hold a thought in my head for more than a few minutes. \n\nLike yesterday, I was trying to review a contract for this incredible spoken-word poet I represent. She's phenomenal, really. She just got approached by this major publishing house to adapt her stage show into a memoir. Which is huge, right? But the contract they sent over had this totally archaic clause about digital rights that I haven't seen since, like, 2012. It reminded me of this time I was negotiating a deal for a voice actor who did all the promotional spots for a regional car dealership network in the Pacific Northwest. The guy who ran the network was this eccentric billionaire who insisted on writing all the copy himself, and he wanted exclusive rights to my client's voice in perpetuity. We ended up having to fly out to Seattle and have this intense sit-down meeting at a seafood restaurant right on the pier. The Dungeness crab was amazing, but the guy kept trying to get us to agree to these ridiculous terms while cracking crab legs, and butter was just flying everywhere...\n\nWait, what was I saying? Oh, right. Focusing. Yeah, no, I just... I can't concentrate on anything. It's like my brain is just constantly short-circuiting.\n\nDoctor: Are you finding it harder to do everyday tasks like shopping or taking care of yourself since your in-laws moved in?\n\nPatient: Everyday tasks? I mean, I guess the shopping gets done eventually, but honestly, it's the bigger things that are completely falling apart. Like at work, I nearly lost this huge contract for a musician I represent. Which is insane for me. He’s this phenomenal jazz pianist, right? Plays the Hammond B3 organ too, which is so rare to see live these days. We had him booked for this massive summer festival in Montreal. Have you ever been to Montreal in July? It's incredible, the whole downtown just turns into this massive street party for the jazz fest. I went there back in my twenties with my college roommate, who actually ended up moving there and opening this little French-Canadian bistro in the Plateau neighborhood. He makes this poutine with duck confit that is just out of this world, we used to stay up until 4 AM drinking cheap wine and arguing about Miles Davis... \n\nWait, sorry. I don't know how she dealt with those tiny chairs and the glue sticks all day... but yeah. I guess I'm just not handling much of anything well right now.\n\nDoctor: Are you avoiding your in-laws or any specific interactions with them since they moved in?\n\nPatient: Avoiding them? I mean, I wouldn't say I'm hiding in the closet or anything, but... yeah, I guess I do try to make myself scarce. Like, if I hear them in the kitchen, I'll just stay in my home office. Which is actually this converted sunroom that we renovated last year. The contractor we used was this guy who also builds custom sets for local theater productions. He had this amazing vision for the space, brought in these reclaimed wood beams from a barn in upstate New York. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Relax at home? I mean, I guess. It's not like I'm pacing the floors or anything. I can sit on the couch and watch TV, even if they're in the room. We actually watched this documentary the other night about the history of the circus. It was fascinating, honestly. Did you know that P.T. Barnum didn't even start his circus until he was in his sixties? Before that, he ran this massive museum in New York that had all these bizarre exhibits, like a supposedly real mermaid and a giant mechanical chess player. The museum actually burned down to the ground—twice! The second time it happened, all the animals got loose in the streets of Manhattan. They had to get the police to wrangle a beluga whale out of a burning tank. And apparently, a tiger was just wandering around Broadway. Can you imagine just walking to get a coffee and coming face-to-face with a Bengal tiger in the middle of the city? 

Sorry, I'm doing it again. What did you ask me? Relaxing. Right. Um, yeah, I don't really have a problem unwinding. It's not like their presence makes me tense or anything. I just sit down and try to turn my brain off, you know?

I guess I'm just not handling much of anything well right now.\n\nDoctor: Are you avoiding your in-laws or any specific interactions with them since they moved in?\n\nPatient: Avoiding them? He worked on all those ridiculous action movies, you know the ones with the massive explosions and the terrible one-liners? He was telling me once about how they used to rig the cars to flip using these giant nitrogen cannons. No one got hurt, thank god, but it completely destroyed this massive ice sculpture and like a mountain of shrimp cocktail. Can you imagine? Um, no, not really. I mean, my brain is definitely a mess right now, but it's not like a broken record or anything. It's more just... blanking out. They held it at this hotel that was supposedly haunted? Like, full-on Hollywood ghost stories. He had this amazing vision for the space, brought in these reclaimed wood beams from a barn in upstate New York. It was fascinating talking to him because he used to do set design for these avant-garde plays in the East Village back in the nineties. He was telling me about this one production where they flooded the entire stage with two inches of water, and the actors had to perform the whole thing barefoot. Talk about dedication, right? \n\nWait, what was the question? Avoiding them? Right. I don't know if it's a conscious thing, but I just... I don't go out of my way to be in the same room with them. Yeah, I completely dropped the ball on the paperwork, and my managing partner actually pulled me aside and questioned whether I could still be trusted with major negotiations. She basically told me I needed to talk to someone because I'm just not functioning. Which is saying a lot, because she used to teach kindergarten, so she literally has the patience of a saint. I don't know how she dealt with those tiny chairs and the glue sticks all day... but yeah. I guess I'm just not handling much of anything well right now.\n\nDoctor: Are you avoiding your in-laws or any specific interactions with them since they moved in?\n\nPatient: Avoiding them? He had this amazing vision for the space, brought in these reclaimed wood beams from a barn in upstate New York. It was fascinating talking to him because he used to do set design for these avant-garde plays in the East Village back in the nineties. He was telling me about this one production where they flooded the entire stage with two inches of water, and the actors had to perform the whole thing barefoot. It's just easier that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeatedly thinking about your in-laws and their presence in your home, even when you're not directly interacting with them?\n\nPatient: Repeatedly thinking about them? No, not really. I mean, when I'm working, I'm working. Or at least I try to be, when my brain isn't completely stalling out on me. Like, I was looking at headshots for this new actor I just signed. Kid is straight out of Juilliard, ridiculously talented. He actually reminds me a lot of this guy I repped back when I was first starting out. He ended up getting a recurring role on this sci-fi show on the Syfy channel. It was one of those shows that got canceled after one season but had this massive cult following? They filmed it up in Vancouver, and I had to go visit the set once. The amount of green screen they use is just wild. You walk in and it's just this massive warehouse painted entirely neon green, and there's a guy in a motion-capture suit holding a tennis ball on a stick, pretending to be a space monster. I've got enough other things going wrong in my head to occupy my time.\n\nDoctor: Are you having trouble connecting or communicating with your wife since your in-laws moved in?\n\nPatient: Trouble connecting? No, I don't think so. We still talk just fine, really. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Affecting my relationships? Um, no, not really. I mean, I still see my friends, I still talk to my colleagues. It hasn't really changed much. Though, speaking of colleagues, one of the guys I work with, David, he just got back from this insane trip to Patagonia. He was showing me pictures the other day. Apparently, he and his wife hiked this massive glacier, and they actually had to wear these spiked shoes—crampons, I think they're called?—just to walk on the ice. He said the ice was this brilliant, glowing blue color because it's so dense it absorbs all the other colors of the spectrum. They even took a boat tour where they saw chunks of ice the size of buildings breaking off and crashing into the water. Calving, they call it. And the sound it makes is supposed to be like a cannon going off. He brought back this little bottle of water that he claimed was melted glacier ice. He was keeping it on his desk like a trophy. I'm pretty sure the cleaning crew threw it away by mistake, though, because I heard him yelling about a missing water bottle yesterday afternoon... 

Anyway, sorry. What was the question? Friends and colleagues? Yeah, no, everything's fine there. Nothing's really changed.

It's solid mahogany, probably weighs as much as a car. The movers were cursing the whole time they were trying to get it up the stairs. It barely fit through the hallway. And the thing is, it doesn't even hold that much! It's mostly just decorative. It reminds me of this prop we had on a set a few years ago. I mean, I'm definitely stressed out, but not like a sense of impending doom or anything. \n\nThough, talking about dread, you know what I was dreading? My mother-in-law's book club meeting. She decided to host it at our house last Thursday. She invited like twelve women over, and she asked me if I could arrange some \"light entertainment\" because she knows what I do for a living. I represent artists, I don't run a catering hall. But anyway, I ended up calling in a favor from this incredible classical guitarist I work with. He actually just finished a residency at the Kennedy Center, which was a huge deal for him because he's entirely self-taught. He grew up in this tiny town in Andalusia, and he learned to play on his grandfather's guitar, which was missing two strings for the first five years he played it. It really shaped his whole fingerpicking style, it's totally unique. So he comes over, right? And he starts playing this gorgeous, intricate flamenco piece, and halfway through, my father-in-law's espresso machine starts doing its automatic descaling cycle...\n\nWait, sorry. What were you asking? Dread? No, no sense of dread. Just, you know, completely overwhelmed by having them in my space.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble focusing on things for as long as you need to?\n\nPatient: Focusing? Yeah, actually, that's... that's been a real problem. I sit down at my laptop and I just stare at the screen. I can't seem to hold a thought in my head for more than a few minutes. \n\nLike yesterday, I was trying to review a contract for this incredible spoken-word poet I represent. She's phenomenal, really. She just got approached by this major publishing house to adapt her stage show into a memoir. Which is huge, right? But the contract they sent over had this totally archaic clause about digital rights that I haven't seen since, like, 2012. It reminded me of this time I was negotiating a deal for a voice actor who did all the promotional spots for a regional car dealership network in the Pacific Northwest. The guy who ran the network was this eccentric billionaire who insisted on writing all the copy himself, and he wanted exclusive rights to my client's voice in perpetuity. We ended up having to fly out to Seattle and have this intense sit-down meeting at a seafood restaurant right on the pier. The Dungeness crab was amazing, but the guy kept trying to get us to agree to these ridiculous terms while cracking crab legs, and butter was just flying everywhere...\n\nWait, what was I saying? Oh, right. Focusing. Yeah, no, I just... I can't concentrate on anything. It's like my brain is just constantly short-circuiting.\n\nDoctor: Are you finding it harder to do everyday tasks like shopping or taking care of yourself since your in-laws moved in?\n\nPatient: Everyday tasks? I mean, I guess the shopping gets done eventually, but honestly, it's the bigger things that are completely falling apart. Like at work, I nearly lost this huge contract for a musician I represent. Which is insane for me. He’s this phenomenal jazz pianist, right? Plays the Hammond B3 organ too, which is so rare to see live these days. We had him booked for this massive summer festival in Montreal. Have you ever been to Montreal in July? It's incredible, the whole downtown just turns into this massive street party for the jazz fest. I went there back in my twenties with my college roommate, who actually ended up moving there and opening this little French-Canadian bistro in the Plateau neighborhood. He makes this poutine with duck confit that is just out of this world, we used to stay up until 4 AM drinking cheap wine and arguing about Miles Davis... \n\nWait, sorry. Why was I talking about Montreal? Oh. Right, the contract. Yeah, I completely dropped the ball on the paperwork, and my managing partner actually pulled me aside and questioned whether I could still be trusted with major negotiations. It was humiliating. \n\nAnd it’s not just work. At home, the conflict with her parents being there has gotten so serious that my wife is the one who pushed for outside help. She basically told me I needed to talk to someone because I'm just not functioning. Which is saying a lot, because she used to teach kindergarten, so she literally has the patience of a saint. I don't know how she dealt with those tiny chairs and the glue sticks all day... but yeah. I guess I'm just not handling much of anything well right now.\n\nDoctor: Are you avoiding your in-laws or any specific interactions with them since they moved in?\n\nPatient: Avoiding them? I mean, I wouldn't say I'm hiding in the closet or anything, but... yeah, I guess I do try to make myself scarce. Like, if I hear them in the kitchen, I'll just stay in my home office. Which is actually this converted sunroom that we renovated last year. The contractor we used was this guy who also builds custom sets for local theater productions. He had this amazing vision for the space, brought in these reclaimed wood beams from a barn in upstate New York. It was fascinating talking to him because he used to do set design for these avant-garde plays in the East Village back in the nineties. He was telling me about this one production where they flooded the entire stage with two inches of water, and the actors had to perform the whole thing barefoot. The lead actor actually slipped during opening night and sprained his ankle, but he just kept going, incorporated the limp into the character. Talk about dedication, right? \n\nWait, what was the question? Avoiding them? Right. I don't know if it's a conscious thing, but I just... I don't go out of my way to be in the same room with them. It's just easier that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeatedly thinking about your in-laws and their presence in your home, even when you're not directly interacting with them?\n\nPatient: Repeatedly thinking about them? No, not really. I mean, when I'm working, I'm working. Or at least I try to be, when my brain isn't completely stalling out on me. Like, I was looking at headshots for this new actor I just signed. Kid is straight out of Juilliard, ridiculously talented. He actually reminds me a lot of this guy I repped back when I was first starting out. He ended up getting a recurring role on this sci-fi show on the Syfy channel. It was one of those shows that got canceled after one season but had this massive cult following? They filmed it up in Vancouver, and I had to go visit the set once. The amount of green screen they use is just wild. You walk in and it's just this massive warehouse painted entirely neon green, and there's a guy in a motion-capture suit holding a tennis ball on a stick, pretending to be a space monster. It's so surreal. And the catering... man, film sets always have the best craft services. They had this whole waffle bar set up...\n\nSorry, what did you ask me? Oh, thinking about my in-laws. No, I don't just sit around dwelling on them when they aren't right in front of me. I've got enough other things going wrong in my head to occupy my time.\n\nDoctor: Are you having trouble connecting or communicating with your wife since your in-laws moved in?\n\nPatient: Trouble connecting? No, I don't think so. We still talk just fine, really. Like just last night we were sitting in the kitchen figuring out dinner, and she wanted to order Thai food from this place we always go to on 4th Street. The owner there is this really fascinating guy, he actually used to be a Hollywood stunt double back in the late eighties and nineties. He worked on all those ridiculous action movies, you know the ones with the massive explosions and the terrible one-liners? He was telling me once about how they used to rig the cars to flip using these giant nitrogen cannons. Apparently, on one shoot, the timing was completely off and the car actually flipped right into the catering tent. No one got hurt, thank god, but it completely destroyed this massive ice sculpture and like a mountain of shrimp cocktail. Can you imagine? Just thousands of dollars of shrimp flying through the air in slow motion while the director is screaming... \n\nAnyway, uh, where was I going with that? Oh, right, communicating with my wife. No, we're good on that front. Her parents being here hasn't really changed how she and I talk to each other, so I wouldn't say I'm having trouble connecting with her.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself having thoughts that repeat over and over in your mind and are difficult to stop?\n\nPatient: Thoughts repeating over and over? Um, no, not really. I mean, my brain is definitely a mess right now, but it's not like a broken record or anything. It's more just... blanking out. Like I was trying to remember the name of this casting director yesterday. I've known her for fifteen years. We actually met at this awful networking mixer in LA back when I was an assistant. They held it at this hotel that was supposedly haunted? Like, full-on Hollywood ghost stories. The bartender was telling us that Marilyn Monroe used to stay in the penthouse and sometimes you could see her reflection in the mirrors in the lobby. I don't believe in that stuff, but my boss at the time was terrified. He refused to use the elevator and made us walk up six flights of stairs. And of course, halfway up, the lights flickered, and he practically jumped out of his skin. He dropped his entire briefcase, and all these headshots went flying down the stairwell...\n\nWait, sorry. What were you asking? Repeating thoughts? No, I don't have anything like that. Just the blanking out, really.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you can unwind and relax at home with your in-laws there?\n\nPatient: Relax at home? I mean, I guess. It's not like I'm pacing the floors or anything. I can sit on the couch and watch TV, even if they're in the room. We actually watched this documentary the other night about the history of the circus. It was fascinating, honestly. Did you know that P.T. Barnum didn't even start his circus until he was in his sixties? Before that, he ran this massive museum in New York that had all these bizarre exhibits, like a supposedly real mermaid and a giant mechanical chess player. The museum actually burned down to the ground—twice! The second time it happened, all the animals got loose in the streets of Manhattan. They had to get the police to wrangle a beluga whale out of a burning tank. And apparently, a tiger was just wandering around Broadway. Can you imagine just walking to get a coffee and coming face-to-face with a Bengal tiger in the middle of the city? \n\nSorry, I'm doing it again. What did you ask me? Relaxing. Right. Um, yeah, I don't really have a problem unwinding. It's not like their presence makes me tense or anything. I just sit down and try to turn my brain off, you know?\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any traumatic events in your past?\n\nPatient: Traumatic events? No, nothing like that. I've had a pretty standard life, really. Grew up in the suburbs, went to school, got a job. Though, speaking of jobs, my first real gig out of grad school was at this boutique talent agency, and the guy who ran it was an absolute terror. He threw a stapler at my head once. A heavy, metal Swingline stapler. He missed, obviously, but it shattered the glass on this framed poster of 'The Godfather' he had on the wall. He was obsessed with Marlon Brando. He actually bought one of Brando's old motorcycles at an auction once, this vintage Triumph. He paid an absurd amount of money for it and then refused to ever ride it because he didn't want to scratch the paint. He just kept it parked in the middle of his living room like a piece of furniture. His wife hated it. She threatened to leave him over it, which is hilarious because he ended up leaving her for an aspiring actress who was like half his age... \n\nAnyway, sorry. No, no trauma. No big events or anything like that. Just regular life stuff.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping since your in-laws moved in?\n\nPatient: Trouble sleeping? No, not really. I mean, I sleep fine once I actually get into bed. I don't lay there tossing and turning or anything. Although, getting into bed is a whole process now. We had to move our entire bedroom setup around to accommodate some of the furniture they brought with them. My mother-in-law has this massive antique armoire that she absolutely refused to part with. It's solid mahogany, probably weighs as much as a car. The movers were cursing the whole time they were trying to get it up the stairs. It barely fit through the hallway. And the thing is, it doesn't even hold that much! It's mostly just decorative. It reminds me of this prop we had on a set a few years ago. We were shooting this period piece, set in the 1800s, and the production designer found this incredible, ornate wardrobe in a thrift store in upstate New York. They paid next to nothing for it, but it looked like it belonged in a palace. We lost half a day of shooting because of a piece of furniture...\n\nWait, what were we talking about? Oh, sleeping. Right. No, my sleep is fine. No issues there.\n\nDoctor: Are you finding that your current situation with your in-laws is affecting your relationships with friends or colleagues outside of work and home?\n\nPatient: Affecting my relationships? Um, no, not really. I mean, I still see my friends, I still talk to my colleagues. It hasn't really changed much. Though, speaking of colleagues, one of the guys I work with, David, he just got back from this insane trip to Patagonia. He was showing me pictures the other day. Apparently, he and his wife hiked this massive glacier, and they actually had to wear these spiked shoes—crampons, I think they're called?—just to walk on the ice. He said the ice was this brilliant, glowing blue color because it's so dense it absorbs all the other colors of the spectrum. They even took a boat tour where they saw chunks of ice the size of buildings breaking off and crashing into the water. Calving, they call it. And the sound it makes is supposed to be like a cannon going off. He brought back this little bottle of water that he claimed was melted glacier ice. He was keeping it on his desk like a trophy. I'm pretty sure the cleaning crew threw it away by mistake, though, because I heard him yelling about a missing water bottle yesterday afternoon... \n\nAnyway, sorry. What was the question? Friends and colleagues? Yeah, no, everything's fine there. Nothing's really changed.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. I mean, I guess the shopping gets done eventually, but honestly, it's the bigger things that are completely falling apart. Like at work, I nearly lost this huge contract for a musician I represent. Which is insane for me. He’s this phenomenal jazz pianist, right? Plays the Hammond B3 organ too, which is so rare to see live these days. We had him booked for this massive summer festival in Montreal. Have you ever been to Montreal in July? It's incredible, the whole downtown just turns into this massive street party for the jazz fest. I went there back in my twenties with my college roommate, who actually ended up moving there and opening this little French-Canadian bistro in the Plateau neighborhood. He makes this poutine with duck confit that is just out of this world, we used to stay up until 4 AM drinking cheap wine and arguing about Miles Davis... \n\nWait, sorry. Why was I talking about Montreal? Oh. Right, the contract. Yeah, I completely dropped the ball on the paperwork, and my managing partner actually pulled me aside and questioned whether I could still be trusted with major negotiations. It was humiliating. \n\nAnd it’s not just work. At home, the conflict with her parents being there has gotten so serious that my wife is the one who pushed for outside help. She basically told me I needed to talk to someone because I'm just not functioning. Which is saying a lot, because she used to teach kindergarten, so she literally has the patience of a saint. I don't know how she dealt with those tiny chairs and the glue sticks all day... but yeah. I guess I'm just not handling much of anything well right now.\n\nDoctor: Are you avoiding your in-laws or any specific interactions with them since they moved in?\n\nPatient: Avoiding them? I mean, I wouldn't say I'm hiding in the closet or anything, but... yeah, I guess I do try to make myself scarce. Like, if I hear them in the kitchen, I'll just stay in my home office. Which is actually this converted sunroom that we renovated last year. The contractor we used was this guy who also builds custom sets for local theater productions. He had this amazing vision for the space, brought in these reclaimed wood beams from a barn in upstate New York. It was fascinating talking to him because he used to do set design for these avant-garde plays in the East Village back in the nineties. He was telling me about this one production where they flooded the entire stage with two inches of water, and the actors had to perform the whole thing barefoot. The lead actor actually slipped during opening night and sprained his ankle, but he just kept going, incorporated the limp into the character. Talk about dedication, right? \n\nWait, what was the question? Avoiding them? Right. I don't know if it's a conscious thing, but I just... I don't go out of my way to be in the same room with them. It's just easier that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeatedly thinking about your in-laws and their presence in your home, even when you're not directly interacting with them?\n\nPatient: Repeatedly thinking about them? No, not really. I mean, when I'm working, I'm working. Or at least I try to be, when my brain isn't completely stalling out on me. Like, I was looking at headshots for this new actor I just signed. Kid is straight out of Juilliard, ridiculously talented. He actually reminds me a lot of this guy I repped back when I was first starting out. He ended up getting a recurring role on this sci-fi show on the Syfy channel. It was one of those shows that got canceled after one season but had this massive cult following? They filmed it up in Vancouver, and I had to go visit the set once. The amount of green screen they use is just wild. You walk in and it's just this massive warehouse painted entirely neon green, and there's a guy in a motion-capture suit holding a tennis ball on a stick, pretending to be a space monster. It's so surreal. And the catering... man, film sets always have the best craft services. They had this whole waffle bar set up...\n\nSorry, what did you ask me? Oh, thinking about my in-laws. No, I don't just sit around dwelling on them when they aren't right in front of me. I've got enough other things going wrong in my head to occupy my time.\n\nDoctor: Are you having trouble connecting or communicating with your wife since your in-laws moved in?\n\nPatient: Trouble connecting? No, I don't think so. We still talk just fine, really. Like just last night we were sitting in the kitchen figuring out dinner, and she wanted to order Thai food from this place we always go to on 4th Street. The owner there is this really fascinating guy, he actually used to be a Hollywood stunt double back in the late eighties and nineties. He worked on all those ridiculous action movies, you know the ones with the massive explosions and the terrible one-liners? He was telling me once about how they used to rig the cars to flip using these giant nitrogen cannons. Apparently, on one shoot, the timing was completely off and the car actually flipped right into the catering tent. No one got hurt, thank god, but it completely destroyed this massive ice sculpture and like a mountain of shrimp cocktail. Can you imagine? Just thousands of dollars of shrimp flying through the air in slow motion while the director is screaming... \n\nAnyway, uh, where was I going with that? Oh, right, communicating with my wife. No, we're good on that front. Her parents being here hasn't really changed how she and I talk to each other, so I wouldn't say I'm having trouble connecting with her.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself having thoughts that repeat over and over in your mind and are difficult to stop?\n\nPatient: Thoughts repeating over and over? Um, no, not really. I mean, my brain is definitely a mess right now, but it's not like a broken record or anything. It's more just... blanking out. Like I was trying to remember the name of this casting director yesterday. I've known her for fifteen years. We actually met at this awful networking mixer in LA back when I was an assistant. They held it at this hotel that was supposedly haunted? Like, full-on Hollywood ghost stories. The bartender was telling us that Marilyn Monroe used to stay in the penthouse and sometimes you could see her reflection in the mirrors in the lobby. I don't believe in that stuff, but my boss at the time was terrified. He refused to use the elevator and made us walk up six flights of stairs. And of course, halfway up, the lights flickered, and he practically jumped out of his skin. He dropped his entire briefcase, and all these headshots went flying down the stairwell...\n\nWait, sorry. Just the blanking out, really.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you can unwind and relax at home with your in-laws there?\n\nPatient: Relax at home? I mean, I guess. It's not like I'm pacing the floors or anything. I can sit on the couch and watch TV, even if they're in the room. We actually watched this documentary the other night about the history of the circus. It was fascinating, honestly. Did you know that P.T. Barnum didn't even start his circus until he was in his sixties? Before that, he ran this massive museum in New York that had all these bizarre exhibits, like a supposedly real mermaid and a giant mechanical chess player. The museum actually burned down to the ground—twice! The second time it happened, all the animals got loose in the streets of Manhattan. They had to get the police to wrangle a beluga whale out of a burning tank. And apparently, a tiger was just wandering around Broadway. Can you imagine just walking to get a coffee and coming face-to-face with a Bengal tiger in the middle of the city? \n\nSorry, I'm doing it again. What did you ask me? Relaxing. Right. Um, yeah, I don't really have a problem unwinding. It's not like their presence makes me tense or anything. I just sit down and try to turn my brain off, you know?\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any traumatic events in your past?\n\nPatient: Traumatic events? No, nothing like that. I've had a pretty standard life, really. Grew up in the suburbs, went to school, got a job. Though, speaking of jobs, my first real gig out of grad school was at this boutique talent agency, and the guy who ran it was an absolute terror. He threw a stapler at my head once. A heavy, metal Swingline stapler. He missed, obviously, but it shattered the glass on this framed poster of 'The Godfather' he had on the wall. He was obsessed with Marlon Brando. He actually bought one of Brando's old motorcycles at an auction once, this vintage Triumph. He paid an absurd amount of money for it and then refused to ever ride it because he didn't want to scratch the paint. He just kept it parked in the middle of his living room like a piece of furniture. His wife hated it. She threatened to leave him over it, which is hilarious because he ended up leaving her for an aspiring actress who was like half his age... \n\nAnyway, sorry. No, no trauma. No big events or anything like that. Just regular life stuff.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping since your in-laws moved in?\n\nPatient: Trouble sleeping? No, not really. I mean, I sleep fine once I actually get into bed. I don't lay there tossing and turning or anything. Although, getting into bed is a whole process now. We had to move our entire bedroom setup around to accommodate some of the furniture they brought with them. My mother-in-law has this massive antique armoire that she absolutely refused to part with. It's solid mahogany, probably weighs as much as a car. The movers were cursing the whole time they were trying to get it up the stairs. It barely fit through the hallway. And the thing is, it doesn't even hold that much! It's mostly just decorative. It reminds me of this prop we had on a set a few years ago. We were shooting this period piece, set in the 1800s, and the production designer found this incredible, ornate wardrobe in a thrift store in upstate New York. They paid next to nothing for it, but it looked like it belonged in a palace. They had this massive screaming match right in the middle of the soundstage. The actor actually stormed off and locked himself in his trailer for three hours. We lost half a day of shooting because of a piece of furniture...\n\nWait, what were we talking about? Oh, sleeping. Right. No, my sleep is fine. No issues there.\n\nDoctor: Are you finding that your current situation with your in-laws is affecting your relationships with friends or colleagues outside of work and home?\n\nPatient: Affecting my relationships? He said the ice was this brilliant, glowing blue color because it's so dense it absorbs all the other colors of the spectrum. They even took a boat tour where they saw chunks of ice the size of buildings breaking off and crashing into the water. Calving, they call it. I don't know if I actively avoid it. I mean, I guess I don't really sit around dwelling on it, if that's what you mean. But it's hard not to think about it when they're right there all the time. Though, speaking of avoiding things, my brother is the king of that. He's been avoiding doing his taxes for, like, three years now. He runs this small landscaping business, mostly just him and a couple of guys he hires during the summer. He does incredible work, though. He completely redesigned this one client's backyard to look like a Japanese Zen garden, complete with a koi pond and a little wooden bridge. He even imported these specific types of moss from Oregon because they wouldn't grow naturally in his climate. The client loved it so much they threw a massive garden party just to show it off. They had string lights everywhere, a live jazz trio, catered sushi... but then my brother gets a letter from the IRS, and instead of dealing with it, he just throws it in a shoebox in his closet. I can't seem to hold a thought in my head for more than a few minutes. \n\nLike yesterday, I was trying to review a contract for this incredible spoken-word poet I represent. She's phenomenal, really. She just got approached by this major publishing house to adapt her stage show into a memoir. Which is huge, right? But the contract they sent over had this totally archaic clause about digital rights that I haven't seen since, like, 2012. It reminded me of this time I was negotiating a deal for a voice actor who did all the promotional spots for a regional car dealership network in the Pacific Northwest. The guy who ran the network was this eccentric billionaire who insisted on writing all the copy himself, and he wanted exclusive rights to my client's voice in perpetuity. We ended up having to fly out to Seattle and have this intense sit-down meeting at a seafood restaurant right on the pier. The Dungeness crab was amazing, but the guy kept trying to get us to agree to these ridiculous terms while cracking crab legs, and butter was just flying everywhere...\n\nWait, what was I saying? Oh, right. Focusing. Yeah, no, I just... I can't concentrate on anything. It's like my brain is just constantly short-circuiting.\n\nDoctor: Are you finding it harder to do everyday tasks like shopping or taking care of yourself since your in-laws moved in?\n\nPatient: Everyday tasks? I mean, I guess the shopping gets done eventually, but honestly, it's the bigger things that are completely falling apart. Like at work, I nearly lost this huge contract for a musician I represent. Which is insane for me. He’s this phenomenal jazz pianist, right? Plays the Hammond B3 organ too, which is so rare to see live these days. We had him booked for this massive summer festival in Montreal. Have you ever been to Montreal in July? It's incredible, the whole downtown just turns into this massive street party for the jazz fest. I went there back in my twenties with my college roommate, who actually ended up moving there and opening this little French-Canadian bistro in the Plateau neighborhood. He makes this poutine with duck confit that is just out of this world, we used to stay up until 4 AM drinking cheap wine and arguing about Miles Davis... \n\nWait, sorry. Why was I talking about Montreal? Oh. Right, the contract. Yeah, I completely dropped the ball on the paperwork, and my managing partner actually pulled me aside and questioned whether I could still be trusted with major negotiations. It was humiliating. \n\nAnd it’s not just work. At home, the conflict with her parents being there has gotten so serious that my wife is the one who pushed for outside help. She basically told me I needed to talk to someone because I'm just not functioning. Which is saying a lot, because she used to teach kindergarten, so she literally has the patience of a saint. I don't know how she dealt with those tiny chairs and the glue sticks all day... but yeah. I guess I'm just not handling much of anything well right now.\n\nDoctor: Are you avoiding your in-laws or any specific interactions with them since they moved in?\n\nPatient: Avoiding them? I mean, I wouldn't say I'm hiding in the closet or anything, but... yeah, I guess I do try to make myself scarce. Like, if I hear them in the kitchen, I'll just stay in my home office. Which is actually this converted sunroom that we renovated last year. The contractor we used was this guy who also builds custom sets for local theater productions. He had this amazing vision for the space, brought in these reclaimed wood beams from a barn in upstate New York. It was fascinating talking to him because he used to do set design for these avant-garde plays in the East Village back in the nineties. He was telling me about this one production where they flooded the entire stage with two inches of water, and the actors had to perform the whole thing barefoot. The lead actor actually slipped during opening night and sprained his ankle, but he just kept going, incorporated the limp into the character. Talk about dedication, right? \n\nWait, what was the question? Avoiding them? Right. I don't know if it's a conscious thing, but I just... I don't go out of my way to be in the same room with them. It's just easier that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeatedly thinking about your in-laws and their presence in your home, even when you're not directly interacting with them?\n\nPatient: Repeatedly thinking about them? No, not really. I mean, when I'm working, I'm working. Or at least I try to be, when my brain isn't completely stalling out on me. Like, I was looking at headshots for this new actor I just signed. Kid is straight out of Juilliard, ridiculously talented. He actually reminds me a lot of this guy I repped back when I was first starting out. He ended up getting a recurring role on this sci-fi show on the Syfy channel. It was one of those shows that got canceled after one season but had this massive cult following? They filmed it up in Vancouver, and I had to go visit the set once. The amount of green screen they use is just wild. You walk in and it's just this massive warehouse painted entirely neon green, and there's a guy in a motion-capture suit holding a tennis ball on a stick, pretending to be a space monster. It's so surreal. And the catering... man, film sets always have the best craft services. They had this whole waffle bar set up...\n\nSorry, what did you ask me? Oh, thinking about my in-laws. No, I don't just sit around dwelling on them when they aren't right in front of me. I've got enough other things going wrong in my head to occupy my time.\n\nDoctor: Are you having trouble connecting or communicating with your wife since your in-laws moved in?\n\nPatient: Trouble connecting? No, I don't think so. We still talk just fine, really. Like just last night we were sitting in the kitchen figuring out dinner, and she wanted to order Thai food from this place we always go to on 4th Street. The owner there is this really fascinating guy, he actually used to be a Hollywood stunt double back in the late eighties and nineties. He worked on all those ridiculous action movies, you know the ones with the massive explosions and the terrible one-liners? He was telling me once about how they used to rig the cars to flip using these giant nitrogen cannons. Apparently, on one shoot, the timing was completely off and the car actually flipped right into the catering tent. No one got hurt, thank god, but it completely destroyed this massive ice sculpture and like a mountain of shrimp cocktail. Can you imagine? Just thousands of dollars of shrimp flying through the air in slow motion while the director is screaming... \n\nAnyway, uh, where was I going with that? Oh, right, communicating with my wife. No, we're good on that front. Her parents being here hasn't really changed how she and I talk to each other, so I wouldn't say I'm having trouble connecting with her.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself having thoughts that repeat over and over in your mind and are difficult to stop?\n\nPatient: Thoughts repeating over and over? Um, no, not really. I mean, my brain is definitely a mess right now, but it's not like a broken record or anything. It's more just... blanking out. Like I was trying to remember the name of this casting director yesterday. I've known her for fifteen years. We actually met at this awful networking mixer in LA back when I was an assistant. They held it at this hotel that was supposedly haunted? Like, full-on Hollywood ghost stories. The bartender was telling us that Marilyn Monroe used to stay in the penthouse and sometimes you could see her reflection in the mirrors in the lobby. I don't believe in that stuff, but my boss at the time was terrified. He refused to use the elevator and made us walk up six flights of stairs. And of course, halfway up, the lights flickered, and he practically jumped out of his skin. He dropped his entire briefcase, and all these headshots went flying down the stairwell...\n\nWait, sorry. What were you asking? Repeating thoughts? No, I don't have anything like that. Just the blanking out, really.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you can unwind and relax at home with your in-laws there?\n\nPatient: Relax at home? I mean, I guess. It's not like I'm pacing the floors or anything. I can sit on the couch and watch TV, even if they're in the room. We actually watched this documentary the other night about the history of the circus. It was fascinating, honestly. Did you know that P.T. Barnum didn't even start his circus until he was in his sixties? Before that, he ran this massive museum in New York that had all these bizarre exhibits, like a supposedly real mermaid and a giant mechanical chess player. The museum actually burned down to the ground—twice! The second time it happened, all the animals got loose in the streets of Manhattan. They had to get the police to wrangle a beluga whale out of a burning tank. And apparently, a tiger was just wandering around Broadway. Can you imagine just walking to get a coffee and coming face-to-face with a Bengal tiger in the middle of the city? \n\nSorry, I'm doing it again. What did you ask me? Relaxing. Right. Um, yeah, I don't really have a problem unwinding. It's not like their presence makes me tense or anything. I just sit down and try to turn my brain off, you know?\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any traumatic events in your past?\n\nPatient: Traumatic events? No, nothing like that. I've had a pretty standard life, really. Grew up in the suburbs, went to school, got a job. Though, speaking of jobs, my first real gig out of grad school was at this boutique talent agency, and the guy who ran it was an absolute terror. He threw a stapler at my head once. A heavy, metal Swingline stapler. He missed, obviously, but it shattered the glass on this framed poster of 'The Godfather' he had on the wall. He was obsessed with Marlon Brando. He actually bought one of Brando's old motorcycles at an auction once, this vintage Triumph. He paid an absurd amount of money for it and then refused to ever ride it because he didn't want to scratch the paint. He just kept it parked in the middle of his living room like a piece of furniture. His wife hated it. She threatened to leave him over it, which is hilarious because he ended up leaving her for an aspiring actress who was like half his age... \n\nAnyway, sorry. No, no trauma. No big events or anything like that. Just regular life stuff.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping since your in-laws moved in?\n\nPatient: Trouble sleeping? No, not really. I mean, I sleep fine once I actually get into bed. I don't lay there tossing and turning or anything. Although, getting into bed is a whole process now. We had to move our entire bedroom setup around to accommodate some of the furniture they brought with them. My mother-in-law has this massive antique armoire that she absolutely refused to part with. It's solid mahogany, probably weighs as much as a car. The movers were cursing the whole time they were trying to get it up the stairs. It barely fit through the hallway. And the thing is, it doesn't even hold that much! It's mostly just decorative. It reminds me of this prop we had on a set a few years ago. We were shooting this period piece, set in the 1800s, and the production designer found this incredible, ornate wardrobe in a thrift store in upstate New York. They paid next to nothing for it, but it looked like it belonged in a palace. The lead actor, he was this total diva, insisted that his character would never use something so \"ostentatious\" and demanded they swap it out for a plain wooden chest. The director was furious. They had this massive screaming match right in the middle of the soundstage. The actor actually stormed off and locked himself in his trailer for three hours. We lost half a day of shooting because of a piece of furniture...\n\nWait, what were we talking about? Oh, sleeping. Right. No, my sleep is fine. No issues there.\n\nDoctor: Are you finding that your current situation with your in-laws is affecting your relationships with friends or colleagues outside of work and home?\n\nPatient: Affecting my relationships? Um, no, not really. I mean, I still see my friends, I still talk to my colleagues. It hasn't really changed much. Though, speaking of colleagues, one of the guys I work with, David, he just got back from this insane trip to Patagonia. He was showing me pictures the other day. Apparently, he and his wife hiked this massive glacier, and they actually had to wear these spiked shoes—crampons, I think they're called?—just to walk on the ice. He said the ice was this brilliant, glowing blue color because it's so dense it absorbs all the other colors of the spectrum. They even took a boat tour where they saw chunks of ice the size of buildings breaking off and crashing into the water. Calving, they call it. And the sound it makes is supposed to be like a cannon going off. He brought back this little bottle of water that he claimed was melted glacier ice. He was keeping it on his desk like a trophy. I'm pretty sure the cleaning crew threw it away by mistake, though, because I heard him yelling about a missing water bottle yesterday afternoon... \n\nAnyway, sorry. What was the question? Friends and colleagues? Yeah, no, everything's fine there. Nothing's really changed.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your current situation and symptoms are significantly interfering with your ability to function in your daily life or maintain relationships?\n\nPatient: Interfering? I brought in some of our biggest talent! Like, remember that indie band that blew up a few years ago? The ones who had that hit song featured in the car commercial? I signed them when they were still playing in dive bars for beer money. We had to do this whole showcase for them in Austin, during South by Southwest. It was a nightmare. The lead singer lost his voice the day before the gig because he stayed up all night drinking tequila with some locals. We had to pump him full of tea and honey, and I literally stood offstage with a humidifier just blasting it at him between songs. They ended up crushing it, though. That commercial paid for his first house. A beautiful mid-century modern place in the hills. He put in a custom recording studio that cost more than my entire college education...\n\nWait, sorry. Functioning. Right. Yeah, so work is bad. And at home... it's not great either. The tension with my in-laws being there is just... it's constant. The conflict has gotten so serious that my wife is the one who pushed me to get outside help. She basically told me I needed to talk to someone because she can't handle how I'm acting anymore. So, yeah. It's definitely interfering.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself trying to avoid thinking or talking about your feelings related to your in-laws moving in?\n\nPatient: Avoid thinking about it? I don't know if I actively avoid it. I mean, I guess I don't really sit around dwelling on it, if that's what you mean. But it's hard not to think about it when they're right there all the time. Though, speaking of avoiding things, my brother is the king of that. He's been avoiding doing his taxes for, like, three years now. He runs this small landscaping business, mostly just him and a couple of guys he hires during the summer. He does incredible work, though. He completely redesigned this one client's backyard to look like a Japanese Zen garden, complete with a koi pond and a little wooden bridge. He even imported these specific types of moss from Oregon because they wouldn't grow naturally in his climate. The client loved it so much they threw a massive garden party just to show it off. They had string lights everywhere, a live jazz trio, catered sushi... but then my brother gets a letter from the IRS, and instead of dealing with it, he just throws it in a shoebox in his closet. He says he's waiting for the \"right time\" to sort it out, whatever that means. I told him he's going to end up in federal prison over some azaleas and a koi pond... \n\nWait, what did you ask me? Distress? Yeah. Yeah, absolutely. It's... it's overwhelming, honestly. I just feel so wound up all the time. Like, I can't even relax in my own house anymore. I keep telling my wife we need one of those, but she says our patio doesn't have the right layout for it, which is ridiculous because if we just moved the teak planters we bought in Portland... wait, what were you asking me? \n\nOh, right, social stuff outside of work. No, I haven't really noticed myself avoiding that. It's mostly just the work calls I can't seem to face right now.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or empty most of the time since your in-laws moved in?\n\nPatient: Sad or empty? No, not really. Honestly, \"empty\" is the last word I'd use, considering how crowded the house feels now. \n\nMy mother-in-law brought over these huge boxes of porcelain figurines when they moved in. Lladro, I think they're called? They're these really pale, delicate little statues. She insisted on putting them on the floating shelves in our main hallway, which is just a terrible idea because our dog, Barnaby—he's a golden retriever mix we rescued a few years ago from this incredible sanctuary up in Marin County, they actually do this whole intensive behavioral matching process before you can even adopt, it's a great organization—but anyway, Barnaby has this massive tail, and he just completely cleared one of the shelves the other day. Shards of porcelain everywhere. I spent an hour trying to vacuum it up, and I actually had to order a special attachment for our Dyson just to get the tiny pieces out of the floor grout... \n\nWait, sorry. What were we talking about? Oh, right, feeling sad. No, I wouldn't say I'm sad or empty. I just feel incredibly wound up and exhausted all the time.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself worrying about things that might happen in the future?\n\nPatient: Worrying about the future? Hmm, not really, no. I mean, I have to plan for the future all the time for my job, obviously. Like right now, I'm trying to route a summer tour for this indie folk band I represent. We were looking at booking them at Red Rocks, which is this incredible amphitheater in Colorado. Have you been? It's a naturally occurring geological phenomenon, the acoustics are just unbelievable. \n\nI went there for a show back in like '04, and it poured rain the entire time, but nobody cared because the vibe was just so electric. We ended up getting stuck in traffic for three hours trying to get out of the parking lot, though, which is the one massive downside to that venue. You really have to plan your exit strategy or you're just sitting there burning gas, and my buddy's clutch actually started slipping because we were on this steep incline... \n\nWait, sorry, what was the question? Worrying? No, I don't really sit around worrying about what might happen later. I'm just completely overwhelmed by what's happening right now in my house.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing any physical symptoms like headaches or stomach issues since your in-laws moved in?\n\nPatient: Headaches or stomach issues? The beans he buys are from this boutique roaster in Brooklyn, which is actually kind of funny because one of my clients—a really talented character actor who just got booked as a series regular on an HBO show, great guy, totally under the radar until now—he actually invested in that exact same roasting company. They do this whole sustainable, direct-trade thing, which is fantastic, but honestly, the packaging is a complete nightmare to open. I actually sliced my thumb on the foil bag last week trying to get it open because my father-in-law couldn't grip the pull-tab... \n\nWait, sorry, what did you just ask me? Oh, right, physical symptoms. No, no headaches or anything like that. I'm just completely drained from everything else.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel like you're on edge or have a sense of dread?\n\nPatient: On edge? Dread? No, I wouldn't say dread. She decided to host it at our house last Thursday. She invited like twelve women over, and she asked me if I could arrange some \"light entertainment\" because she knows what I do for a living. I represent artists, I don't run a catering hall. But anyway, I ended up calling in a favor from this incredible classical guitarist I work with. He actually just finished a residency at the Kennedy Center, which was a huge deal for him because he's entirely self-taught. It really shaped his whole fingerpicking style, it's totally unique. So he comes over, right? And he starts playing this gorgeous, intricate flamenco piece, and halfway through, my father-in-law's espresso machine starts doing its automatic descaling cycle...\n\nWait, sorry. What were you asking? Dread? No, no sense of dread. Just, you know, completely overwhelmed by having them in my space.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble focusing on things for as long as you need to?\n\nPatient: Focusing? Yeah, actually, that's... that's been a real problem. I sit down at my laptop and I just stare at the screen. I can't seem to hold a thought in my head for more than a few minutes. \n\nLike yesterday, I was trying to review a contract for this incredible spoken-word poet I represent.
