We ended up having to fly out to Seattle and have this intense sit-down meeting at a seafood restaurant right on the pier. Oh, right. Focusing. Yeah, no, I just... I can't concentrate on anything. It's like my brain is just constantly short-circuiting.\n\nDoctor: Are you finding it harder to do everyday tasks like shopping or taking care of yourself since your in-laws moved in?\n\nPatient: Everyday tasks? We had him booked for this massive summer festival in Montreal. Have you ever been to Montreal in July? It's incredible, the whole downtown just turns into this massive street party for the jazz fest. He makes this poutine with duck confit that is just out of this world, we used to stay up until 4 AM drinking cheap wine and arguing about Miles Davis... \n\nWait, sorry. Yeah, I completely dropped the ball on the paperwork, and my managing partner actually pulled me aside and questioned whether I could still be trusted with major negotiations. She basically told me I needed to talk to someone because I'm just not functioning. Which is saying a lot, because she used to teach kindergarten, so she literally has the patience of a saint. I don't know how she dealt with those tiny chairs and the glue sticks all day... but yeah. I guess I'm just not handling much of anything well right now.\n\nDoctor: Are you avoiding your in-laws or any specific interactions with them since they moved in?\n\nPatient: Avoiding them? He had this amazing vision for the space, brought in these reclaimed wood beams from a barn in upstate New York. It was fascinating talking to him because he used to do set design for these avant-garde plays in the East Village back in the nineties. He was telling me about this one production where they flooded the entire stage with two inches of water, and the actors had to perform the whole thing barefoot. The lead actor actually slipped during opening night and sprained his ankle, but he just kept going, incorporated the limp into the character. It's just easier that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeatedly thinking about your in-laws and their presence in your home, even when you're not directly interacting with them?\n\nPatient: Repeatedly thinking about them? No, not really. I mean, when I'm working, I'm working. Or at least I try to be, when my brain isn't completely stalling out on me. Like, I was looking at headshots for this new actor I just signed. Kid is straight out of Juilliard, ridiculously talented. He actually reminds me a lot of this guy I repped back when I was first starting out. He ended up getting a recurring role on this sci-fi show on the Syfy channel. It was one of those shows that got canceled after one season but had this massive cult following? They filmed it up in Vancouver, and I had to go visit the set once. The amount of green screen they use is just wild. Um, yeah, I don't really have a problem unwinding. It's not like their presence makes me tense or anything. Though, speaking of colleagues, one of the guys I work with, David, he just got back from this insane trip to Patagonia. He was showing me pictures the other day. Apparently, he and his wife hiked this massive glacier, and they actually had to wear these spiked shoes—crampons, I think they're called?—just to walk on the ice. He said the ice was this brilliant, glowing blue color because it's so dense it absorbs all the other colors of the spectrum. He runs this small landscaping business, mostly just him and a couple of guys he hires during the summer. He does incredible work, though. He completely redesigned this one client's backyard to look like a Japanese Zen garden, complete with a koi pond and a little wooden bridge. Avoiding my feelings? Uh, I don't know. I haven't really noticed if I do that or not. I'm just trying to get through the day, honestly.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your current situation is causing you a lot of distress?\n\nPatient: Distress? Yeah. Yeah, absolutely. We finally found one at this little antique shop in upstate New York. The guy who owned the shop was this retired history professor who specialized in, like, the American Revolution. He talked our ears off for an hour about how the local militia used to train in the field behind his store. He even showed us this old musket ball he found buried in the mud after a heavy rainstorm. He had it displayed in this little velvet-lined box next to the cash register. I ended up buying this vintage brass lamp from him too, just because I felt bad for taking up so much of his time. It doesn't even work, it just sits in the corner of my office gathering dust... \n\nBut, uh, yeah. Distress. It's a lot. Repeating thoughts? Like, urges? No, I don't think so. I mean, unless you count getting a song stuck in your head. Working in music, that happens to me literally every day. I have this one producer client who makes these incredibly catchy, almost annoying pop tracks. He actually went out into the alley behind the studio in Burbank at like three in the morning to record it. The cops almost got called because the security guard at the lot next door thought someone was trying to break into the building. He had to show the cops his studio badge and play them the audio on his phone right there in the alley. It was a whole mess, but the track went viral on social media, so it paid off. 

But, uh, wait. You meant like... bad thoughts? Or urges I can't control? No, nothing like that. My mind is just racing with the in-law stuff and the stress of it all, but no weird repeating urges or anything.
